A ceasefire presented by the [United Nations](31769.md) to the PRC, after the Battle of the Ch'ongch'on River on 11 December, was rejected by the PRC, which was convinced of the PVA's invincibility after its victory in that battle and the wider [Second Phase Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Phase_Offensive).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang1995119–126-224][^cite_note-225] With Lieutenant General [Matthew Ridgway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Ridgway) assuming command of the Eighth Army on 26 December following the death of previous Eighth Army commander [Walton Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walton_Walker), the PVA and the KPA launched their [Third Phase Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Battle_of_Seoul) on New Year's Eve. Using night attacks in which UN fighting positions were encircled and assaulted by numerically superior troops, who had the element of surprise, the attacks were accompanied by loud trumpets and gongs, which facilitated tactical communication and disoriented the enemy. UN forces had no familiarity with this tactic, and some soldiers panicked, abandoning their weapons and retreating to the south.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990117-226] The offensive overwhelmed UN forces, allowing the PVA and KPA to capture Seoul for the second time on 4 January 1951.

These setbacks prompted MacArthur to consider using [nuclear weapons](21785.md) against the Chinese or North Korean interiors, intending radioactive fallout zones to interrupt the Chinese supply chains.[^cite_note-MacArthur-227] However, upon the arrival of the charismatic General Ridgway, the *esprit de corps* of the bloodied Eighth Army revived.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990113-228]



A machine gun crew of Co. A, 24th Infantry Regiment, in Songnimbong, along [Han River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_River_\(Korea\)), 21 February 1951

[^cite_note-229]

UN forces retreated to [Suwon](502693.md) in the west, [Wonju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wonju) in the center, and the territory north of [Samcheok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samcheok) in the east, where the battlefront stabilized.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990117-226] The PVA had outrun its logistics capability and was unable to press on beyond Seoul as food, ammunition, and matériel were carried nightly, on foot and bicycle, from the border at the Yalu River to the battle lines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990118-230] On 25 January, upon finding that the PVA had abandoned their battle lines, Ridgway ordered a reconnaissance-in-force, which became [Operation Thunderbolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Thunderbolt_\(1951\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990121-231] A full-scale advance exploiting the UN's air superiority,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990120-232] concluding with the UN forces reaching the Han River and recapturing Wonju.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990121-231]

Following the failure of ceasefire negotiations in January, the [UN General Assembly](31957.md) passed [UNGA Resolution 498](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_498) on 1 February, condemning the PRC as an aggressor and calling upon its forces to withdraw from Korea.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234]

In early February, the ROK 11th Division ran an operation to destroy guerrillas and pro-DPRK sympathizers in the [South Gyeongsang Province](319775.md).[^cite_note-jd030210-235] The division and police committed the [Geochang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geochang_massacre) and [Sancheong–Hamyang massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sancheong%E2%80%93Hamyang_massacre).[^cite_note-jd030210-235] In mid-February, the PVA counterattacked with the Fourth Phase Offensive and achieved victory [at Hoengseong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hoengsong). However, the offensive was blunted by US [IX Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IX_Corps_\(United_States\)) at [Chipyong-ni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jipyeong-ri) in the center.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990121-231] The US [23rd Regimental Combat Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/23rd_Infantry_Regiment_\(United_States\)) and [French Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Battalion_in_the_Korean_War) fought a [short but desperate battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Chipyong-ni) that broke the attack's momentum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990121-231] The battle is sometimes known as the "[Gettysburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gettysburg) of the Korean War": 5,600 US and French troops were surrounded by 25,000 PVA. UN forces had previously retreated in the face of large PVA/KPA forces instead of getting cut off, but here they stood and won.[^cite_note-Timmons-236]



[US Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Marines) move out over rugged mountain terrain while closing with North Korean forces

In the last two weeks of February, Operation Thunderbolt was followed by [Operation Killer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Killer), carried out by the revitalized Eighth Army. It was a full-scale, battlefront-length attack staged for maximum exploitation of firepower to kill as many KPA and PVA troops as possible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990121-231] Operation Killer concluded with US [I Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Corps_\(United_States\)) reoccupying the territory south of the Han River, and IX Corps capturing Hoengseong.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990122-237] On 7 March, the Eighth Army attacked with [Operation Ripper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Ripper), expelling the PVA and the KPA from Seoul on 14 March. This was the fourth and final conquest of the city in a year, leaving it a ruin; the 1.5 million pre-war population was down to 200,000 and people suffered from food shortages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990122-237][^cite_note-KIMH-182]

On 6 March, MacArthur gave a press conference where he stated "Assuming no diminution of the enemy's flow of ground forces and materiel to the Korean battle area, a continuation of the existing limitation upon our freedom of counter-offensive action, and no major additions to our organizational strength, the battle lines cannot fail in time to reach a point of theoretical stalemate." No one in Washington disputed MacArthur's prediction that a stalemate would develop. But a victory, because of the commitments and risks to achieve it would entail, was no longer considered practical. The preferred course, because it would be consistent with the greater strategy and preparations against the possibility of world war, was to seek a cease-fire and negotiated settlement of Korean issues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMossman1990319-238] On 12 March, Ridgway gave a press conference at his command post at [Yoju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeoju) stating that regaining the 38th parallel would be a "tremendous victory" for the Eighth Army. It would mean the encroachment of communism in Korea had been stopped – exactly what the UNC had set out to accomplish. Conversely, if the Chinese failed to drive the UNC out of Korea, they would have "failed monumentally". In any case, he emphasized, "we didn't set out to conquer China".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMossman1990320-239]

In late April, Peng sent his deputy, [Hong Xuezhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Xuezhi), to brief Zhou in Beijing. What Chinese soldiers feared, Hong said, was not the enemy, but having no food, bullets, or trucks to transport them to the rear when they were wounded. Zhou attempted to respond to the PVA's logistical concerns by increasing Chinese production and improving supply methods, but these were never sufficient. Large-scale air defense training programs were carried out and the [People's Liberation Army Air Force](381445.md) (PLAAF) began participating in the war from September.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006149-240] The Fourth Phase Offensive had not matched the achievements of the Second Phase or the limited gains of the Third Phase. The UN forces, after earlier defeats and retraining, proved much harder to infiltrate by Chinese light infantry than previously. From 31 January to 21 April, the Chinese suffered 53,000 casualties.[^cite_note-241]

On 11 April, Truman [relieved General MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relief_of_Douglas_MacArthur) as supreme commander for several reasons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990123–27-242] MacArthur had crossed the 38th parallel in the mistaken belief China would not enter the war, leading to allied losses. He believed use of nuclear weapons should be his decision, not the president's.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein199469-243] MacArthur threatened to destroy China unless it surrendered. While MacArthur felt total victory was the only honorable outcome, Truman was more pessimistic about his chances once involved in a larger war, feeling a truce and orderly withdrawal could be a valid solution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalberstam2007600-244] MacArthur was the subject of congressional hearings in May and June 1951, which determined he had defied the president's orders and thus violated the [US Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein199479-245] A popular criticism of MacArthur was he never spent a night in Korea and directed the war from the safety of Tokyo.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalberstam2007498-246]



British UN troops advance alongside a [Centurion tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centurion_tank), March 1951

Ridgway was appointed supreme commander, and he regrouped the UN forces for successful counterattacks[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990127-247] while General [James Van Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Van_Fleet) assumed command of the Eighth Army.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990130-248] Further attacks depleted the PVA and KPA forces; Operations [Courageous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Courageous) (23–28 March) and [Tomahawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tomahawk) (23 March) were joint ground and airborne infiltrations meant to trap PVA forces between Kaesong and Seoul. UN forces advanced to the *Kansas Line*, north of the 38th parallel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990131-249]

The PVA counterattacked in April 1951, with the [Fifth Phase Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_spring_offensive), with three field armies (700,000 men).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990131–32-250] The first thrust of the offensive fell upon I Corps, which fiercely resisted in the [Battle of the Imjin River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Imjin_River) (22–25 April) and [Battle of Kapyong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kapyong) (22–25 April), blunting the impetus of the offensive, which was halted at the *No-name Line* north of Seoul.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990133–34-251] Casualty ratios were grievously disproportionate; Peng had expected a 2\:1 ratio, but Chinese combat casualties from 22 to 29 April totaled between 40,000 and 60,000 compared to only 4,000 for the UN: a ratio between 10\:1 and 15\:1.[^cite_note-252] By the time Peng had called off the attack in the western sector on 29 April, the three participating armies had lost a third of their front-line combat strength within a week.[^cite_note-253] On 15 May, the PVA commenced the second impulse of the spring offensive and [attacked the ROK and US X Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Soyang_River) in the east at the [Soyang River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soyang_River). Approximately 370,000 PVA and 114,000 KPA troops had been mobilized, with the bulk attacking in the eastern sector, with about a quarter attempting to pin the I Corps and IX Corps in the western sector. They were halted by 20 May and repulsed, with Western histories designating 22 May as the end of the offensive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990136–37-254][^cite_note-255]

At month's end, the Chinese planned the third step of the Fifth Phase Offensive (withdrawal), which they estimated would take 10–15 days for their 340,000 remaining men, and set the date for 23 May. They were caught off guard when the Eighth Army counterattacked and regained the *Kansas Line* on 12 May, twenty-three hours before the expected withdrawal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990137–38-256][^cite_note-Xiaobing_2014,_p._181-257] The surprise attack turned the retreat into "the most severe loss since our forces had entered Korea"; between 16 and 23 May, the PVA suffered another 45,000 to 60,000 casualties before their soldiers managed to evacuate.[^cite_note-Xiaobing_2014,_p._181-257] The Fifth Phase Offensive as a whole had cost the PVA 102,000 soldiers (85,000 killed/wounded, 17,000 captured), with significant losses for the KPA.[^cite_note-258]

The end of the Fifth Phase Offensive preceded the [UN May–June 1951 counteroffensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_May%E2%80%93June_1951_counteroffensive). During the counteroffensive, the US-led coalition captured land up to about 10 kilometres (6 miles) north of the 38th parallel, with most forces stopping at the *Kansas Line* and a minority going to the *Wyoming Line*. PVA and KPA forces suffered greatly, especially in the Chuncheon sector and at Chiam-ni and Hwacheon; in the latter sector alone the PVA/KPA suffered over 73,207 casualties, including 8,749 captured, compared to 2,647 total casualties of the IX Corps.[^cite_note-259]

The halt at the *Kansas Line* and offensive action stand-down began the stalemate that lasted until the armistice of 1953. The disastrous failure of the Fifth Phase Offensive "led Chinese leaders to change their goal from driving the UNC out of Korea to merely defending China's security and ending the war through negotiations".[^cite_note-260]

### Stalemate (July 1951 – July 1953)



US [M46 Patton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M46_Patton) tanks, painted with tiger heads thought to demoralize Chinese forces

For the rest of the war, the UN and the PVA/KPA fought but exchanged little territory. Large-scale bombing of North Korea continued, and protracted [armistice negotiations](30076555.md#Armistice_discussions) began on 10 July 1951 at Kaesong in the North.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990145,_175–177-261] On the Chinese side, Zhou directed peace talks, and [Li Kenong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Kenong) and Qiao Guanghua headed the negotiation team.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006149-240] Combat continued; the goal of the UN forces was to recapture all of South Korea and avoid losing territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990159-262] The PVA and the KPA attempted similar operations and later effected military and psychological operations to test the UN Command's resolve to continue the war.

The sides constantly traded artillery fire along the front, with American-led forces possessing a large firepower advantage over Chinese-led forces. In the last three months of 1952 the UN fired 3,553,518 field gun shells and 2,569,941 mortar shells, while the communists fired 377,782 field gun shells and 672,194 mortar shells: a 5.8\:1 ratio.[^cite_note-263] The communist insurgency, reinvigorated by North Korean support and scattered bands of KPA stragglers, resurged in the south.

In the autumn of 1951, Van Fleet ordered Major General [Paik Sun-yup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paik_Sun-yup) to break the back of guerrilla activity. The UN's limited offensive (31 August – 12 November) to shorten and straighten sections of the lines, acquire better defensive terrain, and deny the enemy key vantage points, saw heavy fighting by UN forces, with I Corps and X Corps making limited tactical advances against PVA and KPA forces. The campaign resulted in approximately 60,000 casualties, including 22,000 Americans. The intense battles at [Bloody Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bloody_Ridge), [the Punchbowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Punchbowl), and [Heartbreak Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Heartbreak_Ridge) underscored the challenges of penetrating the Chinese "active defense". Despite PVA/KPA losses of 100,000 to 150,000 troops, these were not crippling, and the PVA forces remained resolute. By November, the UN Command abandoned major offensive operations, and the PVA launched counterattacks with some success.[^cite_note-264] From December 1951 to March 1952, ROK security forces claimed to have killed 11,090 partisans and sympathizers and captured 9,916 more.[^cite_note-EB-72]

PVA troops suffered from deficient military equipment, logistical problems, overextended communication and supply lines, and the constant threat of UN bombers. These factors led to a rate of Chinese casualties far greater than the casualties suffered by UN troops. The situation became so serious that in November 1951 Zhou called a conference in [Shenyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenyang) to discuss the PVA's logistical problems. It was decided to accelerate the construction of railways and airfields, to increase the trucks available to the army, and to improve air defense by any means possible. These commitments did little to address the problems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006148-265]



New Zealand artillery crew in action, 1952

In the months after the Shenyang conference, Peng went to Beijing several times to brief Mao and Zhou about the heavy casualties and the increasing difficulty of keeping front lines supplied with basic necessities. Peng was convinced the war would be protracted and that neither side would be able to achieve victory in the near future. On 24 February 1952, the [Military Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Military_Commission_\(People's_Republic_of_China\)), presided over by Zhou, discussed the PVA's logistical problems with members of government agencies. After government representatives emphasized their inability to meet the war demands, Peng shouted: "You have this and that problem ... You should go to the front and see with your own eyes what food and clothing the soldiers have! Not to speak of the casualties! For what are they giving their lives? We have no aircraft. We have only a few guns. Transports are not protected. More and more soldiers are dying of starvation. Can't you overcome some of your difficulties?" The atmosphere became so tense Zhou was forced to adjourn the conference. Zhou called a series of meetings, where it was agreed the PVA would be divided into three groups, to be dispatched to Korea in shifts; to accelerate training of pilots; to provide more anti-aircraft guns to front lines; to purchase more military equipment and ammunition from the Soviet Union; to provide the army with more food and clothing; and to transfer the responsibility of logistics to the central government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006148–49-266]

General [Mark W. Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_W._Clark) replaced General Ridgway as commander of the UN Command on 12 May 1952.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings1988-267]

With peace negotiations ongoing, the Chinese attempted a final offensive in the final weeks of the war to capture territory: on 10 June, 30,000 Chinese troops struck South Korean and US divisions on a 13 kilometres (8 miles) front, and on 13 July, 80,000 Chinese soldiers struck the east-central Kumsong sector, with the brunt of their attack falling on 4 South Korean divisions. The Chinese had success in penetrating South Korean lines but failed to capitalize, particularly when US forces responded with overwhelming firepower. Chinese casualties in their final major offensive (above normal wastage for the front) were about 72,000, including 25,000 killed compared to 14,000 for the UN (most were South Koreans, 1,611 were Americans).[^cite_note-268]

While Chinese forces grappled with significant logistical and supply difficulties, the stalemate also stemmed from mounting frustrations within the UNC. Despite superior firepower, the war proved difficult to fight and the US public was becoming impatient of a war that was lacking a victory. By mid-1951, the stalemate had worn away Truman's [public approval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_approval_rating#:~\:text=Harry_S.,February_9%E2%80%9314,_1952.), and political pressures mounted on the Truman administration to seek an end to the fighting. By October 1952, Van Fleet alleged he was not receiving sufficient ammunition to continue supporting the American involvement, prompting a public controversy. US Secretary of Defense [Robert A. Lovett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_A._Lovett) denied these charges, arguing that shortages were due to army-level logistics rather than a lack of support and mismanagement from the Pentagon.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] On 29 November 1952 US President-elect [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) went to Korea to learn what might end the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990240-271] Eisenhower took office on 20 January 1953 and his administration prioritized containment over rollback and sought to reduce American involvement in the conflict, contributing to the later armistice.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274]

### Armistice (July 1953 – November 1954)

Main article: [Korean Armistice Agreement](30076555.md)



Men from the [Royal Australian Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Australian_Regiment), June 1953

The on-again, off-again negotiations for the armistice spanned over two years (1951–1953),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990144–153-275] first at Kaesong, then [Panmunjom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panmunjom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990147-276] and was the longest negotiated armistice in history.[^cite_note-277] A problematic point was [prisoner of war](25008.md) repatriation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990187–199-278] The PVA, KPA and UN Command could not agree on a system of repatriation because many PVA and KPA soldiers refused to be repatriated back to the north,[^cite_note-Boose2000-279] which was unacceptable to the Chinese and North Koreans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990189–190-280] On 3 December 1952 the [UN General Assembly](31957.md) accepted India's proposed Korean War armistice, voted as Resolution 610(VII),[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-dun1-282] and thus was born the [Neutral Nations Repatriation Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_Nations_Repatriation_Commission).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990242–245-283]

Stalin died on 5 March. The new Soviet leaders, engaged in their internal power struggle, had no desire to continue supporting China's efforts and called for an end to the hostilities.[^cite_note-agov13-284] China could not continue without Soviet aid, and North Korea was no longer a major player. Armistice talks entered a new phase especially after the May 1953 test of the [novel nuclear artillery shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W9_\(nuclear_warhead\)) by the Americans.

The KPA, PVA and UN Command signed the armistice agreement on 27 July 1953.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings1988-267] South Korean President Syngman Rhee refused to sign. The war ended at this point, even though there was no [peace treaty](404505.md).[^cite_note-US_neutral_commission-285] North Korea nevertheless claims it won the war.[^cite_note-Ho1993-286][^cite_note-KCNA2011-287] Under the agreement, the belligerents established the [Korean Demilitarized Zone](328874.md) (DMZ) which mostly follows the 38th parallel. In the eastern part, the DMZ runs north of the 38th parallel; to the west, it travels south of it. Kaesong, site of the initial negotiations, was in pre-war South Korea but is now part of North Korea. The DMZ has since been patrolled by the KPA and the ROKA, with the US still operating as the UN Command.

[Operation Glory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Glory) was conducted from July to November 1954, to allow combatants to exchange their dead. The remains of 4,167 US Army and US Marine Corps dead were exchanged for 13,528 KPA and PVA dead, and 546 civilians dead in UN POW camps were delivered to the South Korean government.[^cite_note-OperationGlory-288] After Operation Glory, 416 Korean War unknown soldiers were buried in the [National Memorial Cemetery of the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Memorial_Cemetery_of_the_Pacific), on [Oahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oahu), Hawaii. [Defense Prisoner of War/Missing Personnel Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Prisoner_of_War/Missing_Personnel_Office) (DPMO) records indicate the PRC and North Korea transmitted 1,394 names, of which 858 were correct. From 4,167 containers of returned remains, forensic examination identified 4,219 individuals. Of these, 2,944 were identified as from the US, and all but 416 were identified by name.[^cite_note-DPMO-289] From 1996 to 2006, North Korea recovered 220 remains near the Sino-Korean border.[^cite_note-Army_Times-290]

### Continued division (1954–present)



Delegates sign the Korean Armistice Agreement in [P'anmunjŏm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panmunjom)

The Armistice Agreement provided for monitoring by an international commission. Since 1953, the [Neutral Nations Supervisory Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_Nations_Supervisory_Commission), composed of members from the Swiss[^cite_note-SwissArmy-291] and Swedish[^cite_note-SwedishArmy-292] armed forces, has been stationed near the Korean Demilitarized Zone.

In April 1975, [South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam)'s capital of [Saigon was captured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Saigon) by the [People's Army of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Army_of_Vietnam). Encouraged by that communist success, Kim Il-sung saw it as an opportunity to invade South Korea. Kim visited China in April 1975 and met with Mao and Zhou to ask for military aid. Despite Pyongyang's expectations, Beijing refused to help North Korea in another war.[^cite_note-KimIlsung1975-293]



US Army officer confers with South Korean soldiers at Observation Post (OP) Ouellette, viewing northward, in April 2008

Since the armistice, there have been incursions and acts of aggression by North Korea. From 1966 to 1969, many cross-border incursions took place in what has been referred to as the [Korean DMZ Conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_DMZ_Conflict) or Second Korean War. In 1968, a North Korean commando team unsuccessfully attempted to assassinate South Korean president [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee) in the [Blue House Raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_House_Raid). In 1976, the [Korean axe murder incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_axe_murder_incident) was widely publicized. Since 1974, four incursion tunnels leading to Seoul have been uncovered. In 2010, a North Korean submarine [torpedoed and sank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROKS_Cheonan_sinking) the South Korean [corvette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corvette) [ROKS *Cheonan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ROKS_Cheonan_\(PCC-772\)), resulting in the deaths of 46 sailors.[^cite_note-BBC2010-294] Again in 2010, North Korea [fired artillery shells on Yeonpyeong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment_of_Yeonpyeong) island, killing two military personnel and two civilians.[^cite_note-Reuters2010-295]

After a new wave of UN sanctions, on 11 March 2013, North Korea claimed that the armistice had become invalid.[^cite_note-296] On 13 March, North Korea confirmed it ended the Armistice and declared North Korea "is not restrained by the north–south declaration on non-aggression".[^cite_note-end_of_armistise-297] On 30 March, North Korea stated it entered a "state of war" and "the long-standing situation of the Korean peninsula being neither at peace nor at war is finally over".[^cite_note-NK_state_of_war-298] Speaking on 4 April, US Secretary of Defense [Chuck Hagel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Hagel) said that Pyongyang "formally informed" the Pentagon that it "ratified" the potential use of a nuclear weapon against South Korea, Japan, and the US, including Guam and Hawaii.[^cite_note-299] Hagel stated the US would deploy the [Terminal High Altitude Area Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_High_Altitude_Area_Defense) [anti-ballistic missile](1791.md) system to Guam because of a credible and realistic nuclear threat.[^cite_note-300]

In 2016, it was revealed North Korea approached the US about conducting formal peace talks to end the war officially. While the [White House](33057.md) agreed to secret peace talks, the plan was rejected because North Korea refused to discuss nuclear disarmament as part of the treaty.[^cite_note-CassellaChiacu-301] In 2018, it was announced that North Korea and South Korea agreed to talk to end the conflict. They committed themselves to the complete denuclearization of the Peninsula.[^cite_note-302] North Korean leader [Kim Jong-un](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Jong-un) and South Korean President [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in) signed the [Panmunjom Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panmunjom_Declaration).[^cite_note-303] In September 2021, Moon reiterated his call to end the war formally, in a speech at the UN.[^cite_note-304]

## Characteristics

The ground war saw [armored offensives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armoured_warfare) with the North's invasion and UN operations, and [tunnel warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnel_warfare) by Chinese forces. The air war saw the first large [jet aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_aircraft) battles, and an early use of US [nuclear deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_deterrence).

### Armored warfare



US soldiers of the [5th Cavalry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_Cavalry_Regiment) examine a captured North Korean [T-34](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-34) tank, [Waegwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waegwan), eastern South Korea

The initial assault by KPA forces was aided by the use of Soviet [T-34-85](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-34-85) tanks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury199014,_43-305] A KPA [tank corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_corps_\(Soviet_Union\)) equipped with about 120 T-34s spearheaded the invasion. These faced an ROK that had few anti-tank weapons adequate to deal with the T-34s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury199039-306] Additional Soviet armor was added as the offensive progressed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerrett1987134–35-307] The KPA tanks had a good deal of early successes against ROK infantry, Task Force Smith, and the US [M24 Chaffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M24_Chaffee) light tanks that they encountered.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein199418-309] Interdiction by ground attack aircraft was the only means of slowing the advancing KPA armor. The tide turned in favor of the UN forces in August 1950 when the KPA suffered major tank losses during a series of battles in which the UN forces brought heavier equipment to bear, including American [M4A3 Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_Sherman) and M26 medium tanks, alongside British [Centurion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centurion_tank), [Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churchill_tank), and [Cromwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cromwell_tank) tanks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990182–84-310]

The Incheon landings on 15 September cut off the KPA supply lines, causing their armored forces and infantry to run out of fuel, ammunition, and other supplies. As a result of this and the Pusan perimeter breakout, the KPA had to retreat, and many of the T-34s and heavy weapons had to be abandoned. By the time the KPA withdrew from the South, 239 T-34s, and 74 [SU-76](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SU-76) self-propelled guns were lost.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerrett1987135-311] After November 1950, KPA armor was rarely encountered.[^cite_note-312] Following the initial assault by the North, the Korean War saw limited use of tanks and featured no large-scale tank battles. The mountainous, forested terrain, especially in the eastern central zone, was poor tank country, limiting their mobility. Through the last two years of the war in Korea, UN tanks served largely as infantry support and mobile artillery pieces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERavinoCarty2003130-313]

### Aerial warfare

Further information: [USAF units and aircraft of the Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USAF_units_and_aircraft_of_the_Korean_War) and [Bombing of North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_North_Korea)

#### Air-to-ground operations

Following the North Korean invasion, the UN Command (UNC) began an extensive bombing campaign against North Korea that lasted until the end of the war in July 1953. It was the first major bombing campaign for the US Air Force (USAF) since its inception in 1947 from the US Army Air Forces (USAAF). The air-to-ground attacks "ranged from the widespread and continual use of firebombing to threats to use nuclear and chemical weapons, finally to the destruction of huge North Korean dams in the last stages of the war."[^cite_note-314]

The first bombing attack on North Korea was approved on the fourth day of the war MacArthur immediately upon request by the Commanding General of the [Far East Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_East_Air_Forces_Bomber_Command) (FEAF), [George E. Stratemeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_E._Stratemeyer).[^cite_note-Kim2012-315] Massive incendiary attacks began in late July.[^cite_note-316] US airpower conducted 7,000 close support and interdiction airstrikes that month, which helped slow the North Korean rate of advance to 3 kilometres (2 miles) per day.[^cite_note-airforcemag1april20-317]

North Korea ranks as among the most heavily bombed countries in history,[^cite_note-318] and the United States dropped a total of 635,000 tons of bombs (including 32,557 tons of napalm) on Korea, more than during the entire Pacific War.[^cite_note-Walkom2010-319][^cite_note-japanfocus.org-320] Almost every substantial building in North Korea was destroyed during the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECumings2005297–98-321][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJager2013237–42-322] The war's highest-ranking US prisoner-of-war, Major General William F. Dean,[^cite_note-Witt2005-323] reported that the majority of North Korean cities and villages he saw were either rubble or snow-covered wasteland.[^cite_note-Cumings2004-324][^cite_note-325] Dean Rusk, a supporter of the war and later US Secretary of State, said the United States bombed "everything that moved in North Korea, every brick standing on top of another". Hydroelectric and irrigation dams were bombed in the later stages of the war, flooding farmland and destroying crops.[^cite_note-Fisher-30] North Korean factories, schools, hospitals, and government offices were forced to move underground, and air defenses were "non-existent".[^cite_note-japanfocus.org-320]



A [307th Bomb Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/307th_Bomb_Wing) B-29 on a day-bombing mission in Korea, c. 1951

According to a [*Chosun Ilbo*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chosun_Ilbo) report in 2001, a report by Soviet ambassador and chief military adviser to North Korea, Lieutenant General V. N. Razuvaev, estimated 282,000 North Korean deaths in bombing raids during the war.[^cite_note-326] In an interview with Air Force historians in 1988, USAF General [Curtis LeMay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_LeMay) commented on efforts to win the war as a whole, including the strategic bombing campaign, saying "Right at the start of the war, unofficially, I slipped a message in 'under the carpet' in the Pentagon that we ought to turn SAC lose with some incendiaries on some North Korean towns. The answer came back, under the carpet again, that there would be too many civilian casualties; we couldn't do anything like that. We went over there and fought the war and eventually burned down every town in North Korea anyway, some way or another, and some in South Korea, too ... Over a period of three years or so we killed off, what, 20 percent of the population of Korea, as direct casualties of war or from starvation and exposure? Over a period of three years, this seemed to be acceptable to everybody, but to kill a few people at the start right away, no, we can't seem to stomach that."[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328]

General Matthew Ridgway said that except for air power, "the war would have been over in 60 days with all Korea in Communist hands". UN Command air forces flew 1,040,708 combat and combat support sorties during the Korean War. The FEAF flew the majority of sorties at 710,886 (69.3% of sorties), with the US Navy performing 16.1%, the US Marine Corps 10.3%, and 4.3% by other allied air forces.[^cite_note-airforcemag1april20-317]

Over the course of the war, USAF [B-29 Superfortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_B-29_Superfortress) heavy bombers flew 20,000 sorties and dropped 220,000 tons in Korea. At first, the bomber was used in [strategic day-bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_bombing) missions, although North Korea's few strategic targets and industries were quickly destroyed. Future B-29 bombing raids were then restricted to night missions, justified as largely in a supply-interdiction role. The B-29 Superfortresses dropped the VB-3 "Razon" (1,000-pound explosives)[^cite_note-329] and the VB-13 "[Tarzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASM-A-1_Tarzon)" (12,000-pound bombs)[^cite_note-330] in Korea, mostly for demolishing major bridges, like the ones across the [Yalu River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalu_River), and for attacks on dams. The aircraft also was used for numerous leaflet drops in North Korea, such as those for [Operation Moolah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Moolah).[^cite_note-331]

A Superfortress of the [91st Strategic Reconnaissance Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/91st_Strategic_Reconnaissance_Squadron) flew the last B-29 mission of the war on 27 July 1953.

#### Air-to-air operations

The Korean War was the first in which [jet aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_aircraft) played the central role in air combat. Once-formidable fighters such as the P-51 Mustang, [F4U Corsair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F4U_Corsair), and [Hawker Sea Fury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_Sea_Fury)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990174-332] — all [piston-engined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocating_engine), propeller-driven, and designed during World War II — relinquished their air-superiority roles to a new generation of faster, [jet-powered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbojet) fighters arriving in the theater. For the initial months of the war, the [P-80 Shooting Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P-80_Shooting_Star), [F9F Panther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F9F_Panther), [Gloster Meteor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloster_Meteor), and other jets under the UN flag dominated the [Korean People's Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_People's_Air_Force) (KPAF) propeller-driven Soviet [Yakovlev Yak-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakovlev_Yak-9), and [Lavochkin La-9s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavochkin_La-9).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990182-333][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerrell200571-334] By early August 1950, the KPAF was reduced to only about 20 planes.[^cite_note-airforcemag1april20-317]

Chinese intervention in late October 1950 bolstered the KPAF with the [MiG-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG-15), one of the world's most advanced jet fighters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990182-333] The USAF countered the MiG-15 by sending over three squadrons of its most capable fighter, the [F-86 Sabre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-86_Sabre), which arrived in December 1950.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990183-335][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerrell200576–77-336] The Soviet Union denied the involvement of their personnel in anything other than an advisory role, but air combat quickly resulted in Soviet pilots dropping their code signals and speaking over the radio in Russian. This known direct Soviet participation was a *[casus belli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casus_belli)* that the [UN Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Command) deliberately overlooked, lest the war expand to include the Soviet Union and potentially escalate into atomic warfare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStokesbury1990182-333]

After the Korean War and to the present day, the US Air Force reported an inflated F-86 Sabre [kill ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss_exchange_ratio) in excess of 10\:1, with 792 MiG-15s and 108 other aircraft shot down by Sabres, and 78 Sabres lost to enemy fire.[^cite_note-Puckett2005-337][^cite_note-338] The Soviet Air Force reported some 1,100 air-to-air victories and 335 MiG combat losses, while China's PLAAF reported 231 combat losses, mostly MiG-15s, and 168 other aircraft lost. The KPAF reported no data, but the [UN Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Command) estimates some 200 KPAF aircraft lost in the war's first stage, and 70 additional aircraft after the Chinese intervention. The USAF disputes Soviet and Chinese claims of 650 and 211 downed F-86s, respectively.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] More modern American estimates place the overall USAF kill ratio at around 1.8\:1 with the ratio dropping to 1.3\:1 against MiG-15s with Soviet pilots but increasing to a dominant 12\:1 against Chinese and North Korean adversaries.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343]

Reports by Lieutenant General [Sidor Slyusarev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidor_Slyusarev), commander of Soviet air forces in Korea, are more favorable to the communist side. The 64th Corps claimed a total 1,097 enemy aircraft of all types during operations, for the loss of 335 aircraft (including lost to enemy ground fire, accidents, etc.) and 110 pilots. Soviet reports put the overall kill ratio at 3.4\:1 in favor of Soviet pilots.[^cite_note-digitalarchive.wilsoncenter.org-344] As reported, effectiveness of the Soviet fighters declined as the war progressed. from an overall kill ratio of 7.9\:1 from November 1950 through January 1952, declining to 2.2\:1 in later 1952 and 1.9\:1 in 1953. This was because more advanced jet fighters appeared on the UN side as well as improved US tactics.[^cite_note-digitalarchive.wilsoncenter.org-344]

Regardless of the actual ratio, American Sabres were very effective at controlling the skies over Korea. Since no other UN fighter could contend with the MiG-15, F-86s largely took over air combat once they arrived, relegating other aircraft to air-to-ground operations. Despite much greater numbers (the number of Sabres in theater never exceeded 150 while MiG-15s reached 900 at their peak), communist aircraft were seldom encountered south of Pyongyang. UN ground forces, supply lines, and infrastructure were not attacked from the air. Although North Korea had 75 airfields capable of supporting MiGs, after 1951, any serious effort to operate from them was abandoned. The MiGs were instead based across the Yalu River in the safety of China. This confined most air-to-air engagements to [MiG Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MiG_Alley). UN aircraft had free rein to conduct strike missions over enemy territory with little fear of interception. Although jet dogfights are remembered as a prominent part of the Korean War, counter-air missions comprised just 12% of [Far East Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_East_Air_Forces) sorties, and four times as many sorties were performed for close air support and interdiction.[^cite_note-airforcemag1april20-317]

The war marked a major milestone not only for fixed-wing aircraft, but also for [rotorcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotorcraft), featuring the first large-scale deployment of [helicopters](8286923.md) for [medical evacuation](146346.md) (medevac).[^cite_note-Kreisher2007-345] In 1944–45, during World War II, the [YR-4 helicopter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikorsky_R-4) had seen limited ambulance duty. In Korea, where rough terrain prevented use of the [jeep](15658.md) as a speedy medevac vehicle,[^cite_note-OliveDrab-346] helicopters like the [Sikorsky H-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikorsky_H-19_Chickasaw) were heavily used. This helped reduce fatal casualties to a dramatic degree when combined with complementary medical innovations such as [Mobile Army Surgical Hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_Army_Surgical_Hospital) (MASH).[^cite_note-Day-347] As such, the medical evacuation and care system for the wounded was so effective for the UN forces that a wounded soldier who arrived at a MASH unit alive typically had a 97% chance of survival.[^cite_note-348] The limitations of jet aircraft for [close air support](600792.md) highlighted the helicopter's potential in the role, leading to the development of the helicopter gunships used in the Vietnam War.[^cite_note-Kreisher2007-345]

### Naval warfare

Further information: [List of US Navy ships sunk or damaged in action during the Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_US_Navy_ships_sunk_or_damaged_in_action_during_the_Korean_War)



To disrupt North Korean communications, [USS *Missouri*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Missouri_\(BB-63\)) fires a salvo from its 16-inch guns at shore targets near [Chongjin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chongjin), North Korea, 21 October 1950

Because neither Korea had a significant navy, the war featured few naval battles. A skirmish between North Korea and the UN Command occurred on 2 July 1950; the US Navy cruiser [USS *Juneau*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Juneau_\(CL-119\)), the Royal Navy cruiser [HMS *Jamaica*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Jamaica_\(44\)) and the Royal Navy frigate [HMS *Black Swan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Black_Swan) fought four North Korean torpedo boats and two mortar gunboats, and sank them. USS *Juneau* later sank several ammunition ships that had been present. The last sea battle of the Korean War occurred days before the Battle of Incheon; the ROK ship *PC-703* sank a North Korean minelayer in the [Battle of Haeju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Haeju) Island, near Incheon. Three other supply ships were sunk by *PC-703* two days later in the Yellow Sea.[^cite_note-Marolda2003-349]

During most of the war, the UN navies patrolled the west and east coasts of North Korea, sinking supply and ammunition ships and denying the North Koreans the ability to resupply from the sea. Aside from very occasional gunfire from North Korean shore batteries, the main threat to UN navy ships was from [magnetic mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_mine). During the war, five US Navy ships were lost to mines: two minesweepers, two minesweeper escorts, and one ocean tug. Mines and coastal artillery damaged another 87 US warships.[^cite_note-350]

### Tunnel warfare

See also: [Tunnel warfare § Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnel_warfare#Korean_War)

Tunnel warfare was favored by Chinese and North Korean forces as a response to US-led aerial attacks. "The Chinese resort to tunnel warfare, and the devastating losses to American soldiers, led to the sealing of tunnel entrances by the UN Command. According to later [prisoner of war](25008.md) interrogations, Chinese officers had killed a number of their own soldiers in the tunnels, because the latter had wished to dig their way out and surrender to the UN Command."[^cite_note-351]

The Chinese [People's Volunteer Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Volunteer_Army) under General [Qin Jiwei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qin_Jiwei) constructed an intricate series of defensive networks, which were composed of 9,000 meters (9,800 yd) of tunnels, 50,000 meters (55,000 yd) of trenches, and 5,000 meters (5,500 yd) of obstacles and minefields.[^cite_note-zhang59-352] This tunnel network proved its use in the [Battle of Triangle Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Triangle_Hill) in October and November 1952, where, despite the US [Eighth Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_Army_\(United_States\)) enjoying complete air and artillery superiority, the Chinese managed to keep the hill and inflict heavy casualties on the Americans.

### US threat of atomic warfare



Mark 4 bomb, seen on display, transferred to the [9th Bombardment Wing, Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Bombardment_Wing,_Heavy)

On 5 November 1950, the US Joint Chiefs of Staff issued orders for the retaliatory atomic bombing of Manchurian PRC military bases, if either of their armies crossed into Korea or if PRC or KPA bombers attacked Korea from there. Truman ordered the transfer of nine [Mark 4 nuclear bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_4_nuclear_bomb) "to the Air Force's [Ninth Bomb Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Bombardment_Wing,_Heavy), the designated carrier of the weapons ... \[and] signed an order to use them against Chinese and Korean targets", which he never transmitted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECumings2005289–92-353]

Many US officials viewed the deployment of nuclear-capable (but not nuclear-armed) B-29 bombers to Britain as helping to resolve the [Berlin Blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Blockade) of 1948–49. Truman and Eisenhower had military experience and viewed nuclear weapons as potentially usable. During Truman's first meeting to discuss the war in June 1950, he ordered plans for attacking Soviet forces if they entered the war. By July, Truman approved another B-29 deployment to Britain, this time with bombs, to remind the Soviets of US offensive ability. Deployment of a similar fleet to [Guam](11974.md) was leaked to the *New York Times*. As UN forces retreated to Pusan, and the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) reported that China was building up forces for a possible invasion of Taiwan, the Pentagon believed Congress and the public would demand using nuclear weapons if the situation in Korea required them.[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354]

As PVA forces pushed back the UN forces from the Yalu River, Truman stated during a 30 November 1950 press conference that using nuclear weapons was "always \[under] active consideration", with control under the local commander.[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354] Indian ambassador [K. Madhava Panikkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kavalam_Madhava_Panikkar) reports "that Truman announced he was thinking of using the atom bomb in Korea. But the Chinese seemed unmoved by this threat ... The PRC's propaganda against the US was stepped up. The 'Aid Korea to resist America' campaign was made the slogan for increased production, greater national integration, and more rigid control over anti-national activities. One could not help feeling that Truman's threat came in useful to the leaders of the Revolution...."[^cite_note-Schnabel-181][^cite_note-Knightley1982-355][^cite_note-Panikkar1981-356]

After his statement caused concern in Europe, on 4 December Truman met with UK Prime Minister and [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) spokesman [Clement Attlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_Attlee), and French Premier [René Pleven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Pleven) to discuss their worries about atomic warfare and its likely continental expansion. The US's foregoing atomic warfare was not because of "a disinclination by the Soviet Union and People's Republic of China to escalate", but because UN allies—notably the UK, the Commonwealth, and France—were concerned about a [geopolitical](163225.md) imbalance rendering [NATO](21133.md) defenseless while the US fought China, who might persuade the Soviet Union to conquer Western Europe.[^cite_note-Schnabel-181][^cite_note-Truman1955-357] The Joint Chiefs of Staff advised Truman to tell Attlee that the US would use nuclear weapons only if necessary to protect an evacuation of UN troops, or prevent a "major military disaster".[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354]

On 6 December after the Chinese intervention repelled the UN armies from northern North Korea, General [J. Lawton Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Lawton_Collins), General MacArthur, Admiral [C. Turner Joy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Turner_Joy), General [George E. Stratemeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_E._Stratemeyer) and Major General [Doyle Hickey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doyle_Overton_Hickey), Major General [Charles A. Willoughby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_A._Willoughby), and Major General [Edwin K. Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Kennedy_Wright) met in Tokyo to plan strategy countering the Chinese intervention; they considered three atomic warfare scenarios encompassing the next weeks and months.[^cite_note-Schnabel-181]

- 1: If the PVA continued attacking in full and the UNC was forbidden to blockade and bomb China, and without Taiwanese reinforcements, and without an increase in US forces until April 1951, then atomic bombs might be used in North Korea.

  [^cite_note-Schnabel-181]

- 2: If the PVA continued full attacks and the UNC blockaded China and had effective aerial reconnaissance and bombing of the Chinese interior, and the Taiwanese soldiers were maximally exploited, and tactical atomic bombing was to hand, then UN forces could hold positions deep in North Korea.

  [^cite_note-Schnabel-181]

- 3: If China agreed not to cross the 38th parallel border, MacArthur would recommend UN acceptance of an armistice disallowing PVA and KPA troops south of the parallel, and requiring PVA and KPA guerrillas to withdraw northwards. The Eighth Army would remain to protect the Seoul–Incheon area, while X Corps would retreat to Pusan. A UN commission should supervise implementation of the armistice.

  [^cite_note-Schnabel-181]

The Pentagon and State Department were cautious about using nuclear weapons because of the risk of general war with China. Truman and his advisors agreed and never seriously considered using them in early December 1950, despite the poor military situation in Korea.[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354]

In 1951, the US escalated closest to atomic warfare in Korea. Because China deployed new armies to the Sino-Korean frontier, ground crews at the [Kadena Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kadena_Air_Base), [Okinawa](22477.md), assembled atomic bombs, "lacking only the essential pit nuclear cores". In October 1951, the US effected [Operation Hudson Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Hudson_Harbor) to establish a nuclear weapons capability. B-29 bombers practiced individual bombing runs from Okinawa to North Korea (using dummy bombs). Hudson Harbor tested "actual functioning of all activities which would be involved in an atomic strike, including weapons assembly and testing, leading, \[and] ground control of bomb aiming". The data indicated that atomic bombs would be tactically ineffective against massed infantry, because the "timely identification of large masses of enemy troops was extremely rare".[^cite_note-Hasbrouck1951-358][^cite_note-Chiefof_Staff1951-359][^cite_note-Watson1998-360][^cite_note-CommandingGeneral1951-361][^cite_note-FarEastCommand-362]

Ridgway was authorized to use nuclear weapons if a major air attack originated from outside Korea. An envoy was sent to Hong Kong to deliver a warning to China. The message likely caused China to be more cautious about potential US use of nuclear weapons, but whether they learned about the B-29 deployment is unclear, and the failure of Chinese offensives that month likely was what caused them to shift to a defensive strategy. The B-29s returned to the US in June.[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354]

Despite the greater destructive power atomic weapons would bring, their effect on determining the war's outcome would have likely been minimal. Given the dispersed nature of PVA/KPA forces, the relatively primitive infrastructure for staging and logistics centers, and the small number of bombs available (most would have been conserved for use against the Soviets), atomic attacks would have limited effects against the ability of China to move forces. Attacking Chinese cities to destroy industry and infrastructure would cause immediate dispersion of the leadership and give propaganda to the communists to galvanize support. Since the Soviets were not expected to intervene with their few atomic weapons, the threat of nuclear exchange was unimportant in the decision not to deploy atomic bombs. Their use offered little operational advantage and would undesirably lower the "threshold" for using atomic weapons against non-nuclear states in future conflicts.[^cite_note-363]

When Eisenhower succeeded Truman in 1953, he was similarly cautious about using nuclear weapons in Korea. [Kenneth Nichols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Nichols) wrote that President Eisenhower "ended the Korean hostilities by discreetly threatening to use atomic weapons if the North Koreans and the Chinese did not agree to a cessation of hostilities".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENichols198712-364] The administration prepared contingency plans to use them against China, but like Truman, he feared doing so would result in Soviet attacks on Japan. The war ended as it began, without US. nuclear weapons deployed.[^cite_note-jstor2538736-354]

## Casualties

About 3 million people were killed in the war, mostly civilians, making it perhaps the deadliest conflict of the Cold War era.[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-19][^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-20][^cite_note-Kim_p.45-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] Samuel Kim lists the Korean War as the deadliest conflict in East Asia, and the region most affected by armed conflict related to the Cold War.[^cite_note-Kim_p.45-365]

### Civilian

Though only rough estimates of civilian fatalities are available, scholars have noted that the percentage of civilian casualties in Korea was higher than in World War II or the Vietnam War, with historian [Bruce Cumings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Cumings) putting civilian casualties at 2 million and [Guenter Lewy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guenter_Lewy) in the range of 2–3 million.[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-19][^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-20] According to one source, 1,658,436 civilians died, meaning a 74% civilian death rate.[^cite_note-mortality-18]

Cumings states that civilians represent at least half the war's casualties, while Lewy suggests it may have gone as high as 70%, compared to his estimates of 42% in World War II and 30 to 46% in Vietnam.[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-19][^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-20] Data compiled by the [Peace Research Institute Oslo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Research_Institute_Oslo) lists just under 1 million battle deaths over the war and a mid-estimate of 3 million total deaths, attributing the difference to excess mortality among civilians from one-sided massacres, starvation, and disease.[^cite_note-368] Compounding this devastation for civilians, virtually all major cities on the Peninsula were destroyed.[^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-20] In per capita and absolute terms, North Korea was the most devastated by the war. According to [Charles K. Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_K._Armstrong), the war resulted in the death of an estimated 12 to 15% of the North Korean population (c. 10 million), "a figure close to or surpassing the proportion of [Soviet citizens killed in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union)".[^cite_note-Armstrong-369]

According to the South Korean Ministry of National Defense, there were over 750,000 confirmed violent civilian deaths during the war, another million civilians were pronounced missing, and millions more ended up as refugees. The UN Command estimated that 5 million Koreans (both north and south) had left their homes and become refugees (including internally displaced) by the summer of 1951.[^cite_note-\:7-370] Over 1.5 million North Koreans fled to the South.[^cite_note-Andrew_C._Nahm_2004_pages_129-130-371]

### Military



Korean War memorials are found in every UN Command Korean War participant country; this one is in [Pretoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretoria), South Africa

See also: [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_in_the_Korean_War), [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Volunteer_Corps_for_Korea), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_in_the_Korean_War), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Volunteer_Army), [Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombian_Battalion), [Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kagnew_Battalion), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Battalion), [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Expeditionary_Force_\(Korea\)), [Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg_in_the_Korean_War), [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regiment_van_Heutsz#Korean_War), [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_in_the_Korean_War), [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea_in_the_Korean_War), [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Expeditionary_Forces_to_Korea), [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand_in_the_Korean_War), [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Brigade), [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Squadron_SAAF#Korean_War), [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_in_the_Korean_War), [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_in_the_Korean_War), and [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_in_the_Korean_War)

#### ROK-UN Losses

South Korea originally reported 137,899 military deaths and 24,495 missing, 450,742 wounded, and 8,343 prisoners.[^cite_note-ROK_Web-22] The South Korean government later stated an official tally of 184,573 killed or missing in action, with about 257,000 military deaths in total from all causes.[^cite_note-Clodfelter2017-372]<sup>: 709 </sup> Historian [Max Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Hastings) estimates South Korean casualties higher at 415,000 killed and 429,000 wounded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings1988407-373]

The United States suffered 33,739 deaths in battle, including those missing and declared dead, and 2,835 non-battle deaths.[^cite_note-2025-11-08-DCAS-374] There were 17,730 other non-battle US military deaths outside Korea during the same period erroneously included as war deaths until 2000.[^cite_note-Vogel-375][^cite_note-amerdead-376] The US government estimates that 103,284 American soldiers were wounded in action,[^cite_note-2025-11-08-DCAS-374][^cite_note-Clodfelter2017-372]<sup>: 664 </sup> with another 10,218 that were captured by Communist forces or went missing in action.[^cite_note-Clodfelter2017-372]<sup>: 709 </sup> 1,789,000 American soldiers served in the war, accounting for 31% of the 5,720,000 Americans who served on active duty worldwide from 1950 to 1953.[^cite_note-2025-11-08-DCAS-374][^cite_note-Fact_Sheet:_America's_Wars-6]

American combat casualties were over 90% of non-Korean UN losses. US battle deaths were 8,516 up to their first engagement with the Chinese on 1 November 1950.[^cite_note-377] The first four months prior to the Chinese intervention were by far the bloodiest per day for US forces, as they engaged the well-equipped KPA in intense fighting. American medical records show that from July to October 1950, the army sustained 31% of the combat deaths it ultimately incurred in the entire 37-month war.[^cite_note-378] 45% of American casualties would be incurred after the first armistice negotiations with the Communists took place.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings1988407-373]

&#x20;Deaths from non-American militaries for the UN Command totaled 3,730, with another 379 missing.[^cite_note-ROK_Web-22]
