The Chinese and North Koreans claimed that about 390,000 soldiers from the US, 660,000 soldiers from South Korea and 29,000 other UN soldiers were "eliminated" from the battlefield.[^cite_note-xu-12]

#### Communist Losses

Chinese sources reported that the PVA suffered 114,000 battle deaths, 21,000 deaths from wounds, 13,000 deaths from illness, 340,000 wounded, and 7,600 missing. 7,110 Chinese POWs were repatriated to China.[^cite_note-xu-12] In 2010, the Chinese government revised its official tally of war losses to 183,108 dead (114,084 in combat, 70,000 deaths from wounds, illness and other causes) and 25,621 missing, a total of 208,729 dead or missing.[^cite_note-china.org.cn-387][^cite_note-388] In 2020, Chinese [state media](20803507.md) outlet *[China Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Daily)* reported the number of Chinese soldiers killed in the war at 197,653.[^cite_note-389] Chinese soldiers who served in Korea faced a greater chance of being killed or wounded than those who [served in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War) or the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War).[^cite_note-390]

According to the South Korean Ministry of National Defense, North Korean military losses totaled 294,151 dead, 91,206 missing, and 229,849 wounded, giving North Korea the highest military deaths of any belligerent in absolute and relative terms.[^cite_note-Andrew_C._Nahm_2004_pages_129-130-371] The PRIO Battle Deaths Dataset gave a similar figure for North Korean military deaths of 316,579.[^cite_note-PRIO-391] Chinese sources reported similar figures for the North Korean military of 290,000 "casualties" and 90,000 captured.[^cite_note-xu-12] The financial cost for North Korea was massive indirect losses and lost economic activity; the country was devastated by the cost of the war and the [American bombing campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_North_Korea), which, among other things, destroyed 85% of North Korea's buildings and 95% of its power generation.[^cite_note-392] The Soviet Union suffered 299 dead, with 335 planes lost.[^cite_note-Krivosheev1997-393]

The Americans claimed during the war that they had killed over 1,500,000 Chinese and North Korean soldiers. Historian [Max Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Hastings) states that this figure is likely exaggerated, with a more realistic number being around 500,000 dead Chinese soldiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHastings1988407-373] Historian [Hans van de Ven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_van_de_Ven) writes that reasonable estimates for Chinese and North Korean military casualties are roughly half a million casualties for each of their respective armies, or one million total.[^cite_note-394]

The American [Department of Defense](7279897.md) estimated the PVA suffered about 400,000 killed and 486,000 wounded, while the KPA suffered 215,000 killed, 303,000 wounded, and over 101,000 captured or missing.[^cite_note-Hickey-395] Cumings claims a much higher figure of 900,000 dead Chinese soldiers.[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-19]

Overall, 73% of Chinese infantry troops served in Korea (25 of 34 armies, or 79 of 109 infantry divisions, were rotated in). More than 52% of the Chinese air force, 55% of the tank units, 67% of the artillery divisions, and 100% of the railroad engineering divisions were sent to Korea as well.[^cite_note-396] China spent over 10 billion yuan on the war (roughly US\$3.3 billion), not counting USSR aid.[^cite_note-Xiaobing_2009,_p._112-397] This included US\$1.3 billion in money owed to the Soviet Union by the end of it. This was a relatively large cost, as China had only 4% of the national income of the United States.[^cite_note-xu-12] Spending on the war constituted 34–43% of China's annual government budget from 1950 to 1953, depending on the year.[^cite_note-Xiaobing_2009,_p._112-397] Despite its underdeveloped economy, Chinese military spending was the world's fourth largest globally for the war's duration after that of the US, the Soviet Union, and the UK.[^cite_note-398]

## Violations of the laws of war

Main article: [War crimes in the Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Korean_War)

There were numerous atrocities and massacres of civilians throughout the war committed by all sides, starting in its first days, though the overwhelming majority of such atrocities have been attributed to acts by the South and the US.[^cite_note-399]

### War crimes



South Korean soldiers walk among the bodies of political prisoners executed near Daejon, July 1950

In 2005–2010, a [South Korean Truth and Reconciliation Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth_and_Reconciliation_Commission_\(South_Korea\)) investigated atrocities and other human rights violations through much of the 20th century, from the [Japanese colonial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_colonial_empire) through the Korean War and beyond. It excavated some mass graves from the Bodo League massacres and confirmed the general outlines of those political executions. Of the Korean War-era massacres the commission was petitioned to investigate, 82% were perpetrated by South Korean forces, with 18% perpetrated by North Korean forces.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401][^cite_note-bloodbath-402] The [Bodo League massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodo_League_massacre) were the mass killings of alleged communists by South Korean forces in the summer of 1950 following North Korea's invasion. The commission was able to verify 4,934 execution victims in these massacres but historians estimate that at least 60,000 people were killed with estimates reaching 200,000.[^cite_note-bloodbath-402] The most well known North Korean massacre was the [Seoul National University Hospital massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul_National_University_Hospital_massacre) on 28 June 1950 where over 1,000 patients and wounded soldiers were killed by North Korean soldiers.[^cite_note-403]

North Korean soldiers and officials systematically murdered South Korean civilians throughout the war, as well as conducting a campaign of [mass kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean_abductions_of_South_Koreans) and illegal impressment of civilians in occupied territory. The massacres committed by North Korea were sometimes carried out through public executions using "people's trials" and other times through sporadic bursts of violence by occupying soldiers. The number of victims is estimated in the hundreds of thousands.[^cite_note-404] The Truth and Reconciliation Commission established a minimum of 129,000 South Korean civilians killed by the KPA. Additionally, of the 70,000 South Korean prisoners of war held by the North Koreans, only 8,000 were ever returned. [R. J. Rummel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._J._Rummel) estimated that North Korean [democide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democide) killed 363,000 South Koreans during the war.[^cite_note-405]



Civilians killed during a night battle near Yongsan, August 1950

The commission also received petitions alleging more than 200 large-scale killings of South Korean civilians by the US military during the war, mostly air attacks. It confirmed several such cases, including refugees crowded into a cave attacked with napalm bombs, which survivors said killed 360 people, and an air attack that killed 197 refugees gathered in a field in the far south. It recommended South Korea seek reparations from the United States, but in 2010, a reorganized commission under a new, conservative government concluded that most US mass killings resulted from "military necessity", while in a small number of cases, they concluded, the US military had acted with "low levels of unlawfulness", but the commission recommended against seeking reparations.[^cite_note-bloodbath-402]

The UN bombing of North Korea during the Korean War has been condemned as a war crime by various authors. A Soviet report estimated that 282,000 North Korean civilians were killed in the bombing.[^cite_note-406] Historian Bruce Cumings has stated: "What hardly any Americans know or remember is that we carpet-bombed the North for three years with next to no concern for civilian casualties."[^cite_note-\:2-407] Scholar Sahr Conway-Lanz has stated that, "During the war, American military and civilian officials stretched the term '[military target](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimate_military_target)' to include virtually all human-made structures, capitalizing on the vague [distinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distinction_\(law\)) between the military and civilian segments of an enemy society. They came to apply the logic of [total war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_war) to the destruction of the civil infrastructure in North Korea."[^cite_note-ConwayLanz2006-213]<sup>: 84 </sup> Cumings wrote "The United Nations Genocide Convention defined the term as acts committed 'with intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnically, racial or religious group'. This would include deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about its physical destruction in whole or in part. It was approved in 1948 and entered into force in 1951—just as the USAF was inflicting genocide, under this definition and under the aegis of the United Nations Command, on the citizens of North Korea."[^cite_note-408] Cumings has been accused of engaging in apologia for North Korea, exaggerating American crimes, denying North Korean responsibility for starting the war, and [whitewashing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewashing_\(communications\)) the conditions of the country's *[kwanliso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwanliso)* concentration camps.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410]

&#x20;\


#### No Gun Ri massacre

Almost a half century after the Korean War, on 29 September 1999, a team of [Associated Press](18935732.md) journalists reported that a dozen ex-American GI soldiers had confirmed that US forces killed large numbers of [civilian](145804.md) [refugees](45547.md) – up to 400, mostly women, children and elderly people – in July 1950 under and around a railroad trestle in the hamlet of [No Gun Ri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Gun_Ri_massacre).[^cite_note-\:2-407] The AP "also published declassified military documents showing that US military commanders had issued standing orders to shoot civilians".[^cite_note-\:2-407] The highest levels of the South Korean and US governments ordered and conducted lengthy investigations on the matter. "The Pentagon's No Gun Ri review spent a year examining over a million documents and the interview statements from approximately 275 American and Korean witnesses and issued its findings in early 2001."[^cite_note-ConwayLanz2006-213] In its final report, after years of dismissing the story of Korean villagers, the US Army affirmed the AP's finding that American troops had killed the refugees.

## Environmental impact

This section is an excerpt from [Environmental impact of war § Korean War (1950-1953)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_war#Korean_War_\(1950-1953\)).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Environmental_impact_of_war\&action=edit)]

The Korean War also resulted in severe damage to the environment and ecosystem. During the war, widespread devastation resulted from direct combat, industrial chaos, and the considerable use of chemical agents, such as defoliants. In addition to conventional weapons, chemical agents were often used, killing much flora and fauna, contaminating soil, and destroying forests. Near the end of the war, the U.S Air force bombed five out of the 20 hydroelectric and irrigation dams in North Korea in Toksan, Chasan, Kuwonga, Kusong, and Toksang. These attacks harmed access to infrastructure and washed away important topsoil needed to grow crops like rice. The attacks occurred during the rice growing season, which lasts from May to August, making the effects of the attacks more severe for the local population.[^cite_note-411]

## Aftermath

Main articles: [Aftermath of the Korean War](12243680.md), [Proposed Peace Treaty on the Korean Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_Peace_Treaty_on_the_Korean_Peninsula), [2018–19 Korean peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_Korean_peace_process), [Panmunjom Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panmunjom_Declaration), and [Korean peace agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_peace_agreement)

### North Korea



The [Korean Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Peninsula) at night, shown in a 2017 composite photograph from [NASA](18426568.md)

UN bombardment had "destroyed or forced underground virtually all of the north's heavy industry."[^cite_note-\:6-412] "By the end of the war, North Korea's industrial infrastructure had been knocked out. Electricity generation was only 17.2 percent of its 1949 output; coal was 17.7 percent; steel was 2.8 percent; and cement was 5 percent."[^cite_note-Pembroke2018-214]

After the armistice, Kim Il Sung requested Soviet economic and industrial assistance. In September 1953, the Soviet government agreed to "cancel or postpone repayment for all ... outstanding debts," and promised to grant North Korea one billion [rubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubles) in monetary aid, industrial equipment and consumer goods. Eastern European members of the [Soviet Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Bloc) also contributed with "logistical support, technical aid, \[and] medical supplies." China canceled North Korea's war debts, provided 800 million [yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_yuan), promised trade cooperation and sent in thousands of troops to rebuild damaged infrastructure.[^cite_note-japanfocus.org-320] Contemporary North Korea remains underdeveloped,[^cite_note-413] and continues to be a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) dictatorship since the end of the war, with an elaborate [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean_cult_of_personality) around the [Kim dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_family_\(North_Korea\)).[^cite_note-\:9-414][^cite_note-\:10-415][^cite_note-britannica-416]

Present-day North Korea follows *[Songun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songun)*, or "military-first" policy and has the [highest number of military and paramilitary personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_troops) in the world, with 7,769,000 active, reserve, and paramilitary personnel, or approximately 30% of its population. Its active-duty army of 1.28 million is the fourth largest in the world, after China, the US, and India; consisting of 4.9% of its population. North Korea [possesses nuclear weapons](106424.md). A 2014 UN inquiry into abuses of [human rights in North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_North_Korea) concluded that, "the gravity, scale and nature of these violations reveal a state that does not have any parallel in the contemporary world," with [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) holding similar views.[^cite_note-Amnesty_International_2007-417][^cite_note-ohchr.org-418][^cite_note-hrw1-419][^cite_note-hrw2-420]

### South Korea

South Korea gained a net total of 4,000 square kilometers and some 360,000 citizens from the truce settlement. During the war 650,000 Koreans permanently fled from North to South while 290,000 fled (or [were abducted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean_abductions_of_South_Koreans)) from South to North. These 650,000 joined another 740,000 Koreans who had migrated North to South between 1945 and the start of the war.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422]

By August 1952, the Republic of Korea's Office of Public Information reported that "\$954,659,023 in civilian property damage, including the complete destruction of over 400,000 civilian houses and 8,700 primary schools, had occurred in the war."[^cite_note-\:7-370]

"The US Eighth Army, which arrived in South Korea in 1950, has never left." As of 2018, "almost 30,000 American service personnel remain stationed across South Korea," and are there are more than 80 American military installations in South Korea maintained by the US military.[^cite_note-423]

Postwar recovery was different in the two Koreas. South Korea, which started from a far lower industrial base than North Korea (the latter contained 80% of Korea's heavy industry in 1945),[^cite_note-Robinson_119-120-29] stagnated in the first postwar decade. In 1953, South Korea, and the United States signed a [Mutual Defense Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_Defense_Treaty_\(United_States%E2%80%93South_Korea\)). South Korean [anti-Americanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-American_sentiment_in_Korea) after the war was fueled by the presence and behavior of [US Forces Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Forces_Korea) military personnel and US support for Park's authoritarian regime, a fact still evident during the country's democratic transition in the 1980s.[^cite_note-NYTimes12July1987-424] However, anti-Americanism has significantly declined in South Korea, from 46% favorable in 2003 to 74% favorable in 2011,[^cite_note-425] making South Korea one of the most pro-US countries.[^cite_note-426]

A large number of [mixed-race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerasian) "GI babies" (offspring of US and other UN soldiers and Korean women) were filling up the country's orphanages. Because Korean traditional society places significant weight on paternal family ties, bloodlines, and purity of race, children of mixed race or those without fathers are not easily accepted in South Korean society. International adoption of Korean children began in 1954.[^cite_note-Jang1998-427] The [Immigration Act of 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1952) legalized the [naturalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization) of non-Blacks and non-Whites as US citizens and made possible the entry of military spouses and children from South Korea. With the passage of the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1965), which substantially changed US immigration policy toward non-Europeans, [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_American) became one of the fastest-growing [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) groups in the United States.[^cite_note-Choe2005-428]

### United States

The United States spent US\$30 billion on the war.[^cite_note-429] "By 1951 the United States was spending \$650 billion on defense in current dollars."[^cite_note-430]

One of the legacies of the Korean War is the new and unprecedented American [military-industrial complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military%E2%80%93industrial_complex) that arose in the 1950s. Regarding this legacy, historian Bruce Cumings has stated that: The Korean War was the crisis that, in Acheson's words, "came along and saved us;" by that he meant that it enabled the final approval of NSC 68 and passage through Congress of a quadrupling of American defense spending. More than that, it was this war and not [World War II](32927.md) that occasioned the enormous foreign [military base](843566.md) structure and the domestic military-industrial complex to service it and which has come to define the sinews of American [global power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_\(international_relations\)) ever since.[^cite_note-431]

"The war gave oxygen to the [Truman doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine); removed the postwar cap on military spending; restored and enlarged the American military apparatus after nearly five years of demobilisation; \[and] unlocked the riches of [NSC-68](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSC_68)."[^cite_note-432] "The decision to intervene in Korea did not merely halt and reverse the process of demobilisation, it had a longer term effect." Since 1950, the trajectory of increasing defence spending by the United States, has "continued almost unabated, spiraling upward, despite brief periodic reductions that have done nothing to alter the long-term trend."[^cite_note-433]

According to scholar Louis Fisher, "President Harry Truman's commitment of US troops to Korea in June 1950 is the single most important precedent for the executive use of military force without congressional authority."

### China



North Koreans touring the [Museum of American War Atrocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinchon_Museum_of_American_War_Atrocities) in 2009

Mao Zedong's decision to take on the US was a direct attempt to confront what the communist bloc viewed as the strongest anti-communist power in the world, undertaken at a time when the Chinese communist regime was still consolidating its own power. Mao supported intervention not to save North Korea, but because he believed that a military conflict with the US was inevitable after the US entered the war, and to appease the Soviet Union to secure military dispensation and achieve Mao's goal of making China a major world military power. Mao was equally ambitious in improving his own prestige inside the communist international community. In his later years, Mao believed that Stalin only gained a positive opinion of him after China's entrance into the Korean War. Inside mainland China, the war improved the long-term prestige of Mao, Zhou, and Peng, allowing the Chinese Communist Party to increase its legitimacy while weakening anti-communist dissent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006150-434]

In Chinese media, the Chinese war effort is considered as an example of China engaging the strongest power in the world with an underequipped army, forcing it to retreat, and fighting it to a military stalemate. These successes were contrasted with China's [historical humiliations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_of_humiliation) by Japan and by Western powers over the previous hundred years, highlighting the abilities of the PLA and the Chinese Communist Party.

The Korean War cemented China's relationship with the USSR for some time, making it more dependent on the Soviet Union for development aid and technology, while limiting cooperation between China and the US. A high proportion of government expenditures were needed for the war and this slowed China's reconstruction.[^cite_note-435] The most significant negative long-term consequence of the war for China was that it led the United States to guarantee the safety of [Chiang Kai-shek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek)'s regime in Taiwan, effectively ensuring that Taiwan would remain outside of PRC control through the present day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnouinYu2006150-434] [Anti-US sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Americanism), which were already a significant factor during the Chinese Civil War, were ingrained into Chinese culture during the [communist propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_propaganda) campaigns of the Korean War.[^cite_note-436]

### Others

The Korean War affected other participant combatants. [Turkey](11125639.md), for example, entered NATO in 1952,[^cite_note-StateDept-437] and the foundation was laid for bilateral diplomatic and trade [relations with South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korea%E2%80%93Turkey_relations).[^cite_note-Turquie2010-438] The war also played a role in the [refugee crisis in Turkey in 1950–1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Turks_from_Bulgaria_\(1950%E2%80%931951\)).

[^cite_note-mortality-18]: &#x20;Khorram-Manesh, Amir; Burkle, Frederick M.; Goniewicz, Krzysztof; Robinson, Yohan (2021). ["Estimating the Number of Civilian Casualties in Modern Armed Conflicts–A Systematic Review"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8581199). *[Frontiers in Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontiers_in_Public_Health)*. **9** (1) 765261. [Bibcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibcode_\(identifier\)):[2021FrPH....965261K](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2021FrPH....965261K). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.3389/fpubh.2021.765261](https://doi.org/10.3389%2Ffpubh.2021.765261). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [8581199](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8581199). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [34778192](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34778192).

[^cite_note-Cumings_p._35-19]: &#x20;[Cumings, Bruce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Cumings) (2011). *The Korean War: A History*. [Modern Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Library). p. 35. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780812978964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780812978964). Various encyclopedias state that the countries involved in the three-year conflict suffered a total of more than 4 million casualties, of which at least 2 million were civilians—a higher percentage than in World War II or Vietnam. A total of 36,940 Americans lost their lives in the Korean theater; of these, 33,665 were killed in action, while 3,275 died there of non-hostile causes. Some 92,134 Americans were wounded in action, and decades later, 8,176 were still reported as missing. South Korea sustained 1,312,836 casualties, including 415,004 dead. Casualties among other UN allies totaled 16,532, including 3,094 dead. Estimated North Korean casualties numbered 2 million, including about one million civilians and 520,000 soldiers. An estimated 900,000 Chinese soldiers lost their lives in combat.

[^cite_note-Lewy_pp._450-453-20]: &#x20;[Lewy, Guenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guenter_Lewy) (1980). [*America in Vietnam*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_in_Vietnam). [Oxford University Press](48518.md). pp. 450–453. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780199874231](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780199874231). For the Korean War the only hard statistic is that of American military deaths, which included 33,629 battle deaths and 20,617 who died of other causes. The North Korean and Chinese Communists never published statistics of their casualties. The number of South Korean military deaths has been given as in excess of 400,000; the South Korean Ministry of Defense puts the number of killed and missing at 281,257. Estimates of communist troops killed are about one-half million. The total number of Korean civilians who died in the fighting, which left almost every major city in North and South Korea in ruins, has been estimated at between 2 and 3 million. This adds up to almost 1 million military deaths and a possible 2.5 million civilians who were killed or died as a result of this extremely destructive conflict. The proportion of civilians killed in the major wars of this century (and not only in the major ones) has thus risen steadily. It reached about 42 percent in World War II and may have gone as high as 70 percent in the Korean War. ... we find that the ratio of civilian to military deaths \[in Vietnam] is not substantially different from that of World War II and is well below that of the Korean War.

[^cite_note-Kim_p.45-365]: &#x20;Kim, Samuel S. (2014). "The Evolving Asian System". *International Relations of Asia*. [Rowman & Littlefield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowman_&_Littlefield). p. 45. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9781442226418](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9781442226418). With three of the four major Cold War fault lines—divided Germany, divided Korea, divided China, and divided Vietnam—East Asia acquired the dubious distinction of having engendered the largest number of armed conflicts resulting in higher fatalities between 1945 and 1994 than any other region or sub-region. Even in Asia, while Central and South Asia produced a regional total of 2.8 million in human fatalities, East Asia's regional total is 10.4 million including the Chinese Civil War (1 million), the Korean War (3 million), the Vietnam War (2 million), and the [Pol Pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pol_Pot) [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_genocide) in Cambodia (1 to 2 million).

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9781139486224](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9781139486224). In Korea, war in the early 1950s cost nearly 3 million lives, including nearly a million civilian dead in South Korea.

[^cite_note-367]: &#x20;[Painter, David S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_S._Painter) (1999). [*The Cold War: An International History*](https://archive.org/details/coldwarinternati00pain). [Routledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge). p. [30](https://archive.org/details/coldwarinternati00pain/page/n40). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780415153164](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780415153164). Before it ended, the Korean War cost over 3 million people their lives, including over 50,000 U.S. servicemen and women, and a much higher number of Chinese and Korean lives. The war also set in motion a number of changes that led to the militarization and intensification of the Cold War.

The number of Korean dead, injured or missing by war's end approached three million, ten percent of the overall population. The majority of those killed were in the North, which had half of the population of the South; although the DPRK does not have official figures, possibly twelve to fifteen percent of the population was killed in the war, a figure close to or surpassing the proportion of Soviet citizens killed in World War II.
